CAT VARC Chapter 09: Para Jumbles

Verbal Ability · 350 questions · Teacher book

Paper Tree Educational Studio · v1.0.0

CAT-VARC-C09-001 · Para jumble · EasyTITA
The four sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3 and 4) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of four numbers as your answer.

1.Suppose a popular school has one remaining place and two applicants whose claims to it are, by every relevant measure, exactly equal.

2.Giving the place to either child on the strength of some further feature, such as which parent argued more forcefully, would reward something that has no bearing on the claim.

3.Its fairness, in other words, lies not in the outcome, which still disappoints one family, but in the way the outcome was reached.

4.A lottery avoids this, because it gives each applicant the same chance and lets no irrelevant feature decide.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 1243

Sentence 1 sets up the case ('Suppose') of one place and two equal claims. Sentence 2 refers to 'either child' from that case and shows what is wrong with deciding by a further feature. Sentence 4 says 'A lottery avoids this', where 'this' is the problem of rewarding an irrelevant feature described in 2. Sentence 3 explains 'Its fairness', the lottery's, in general terms ('in other words').

Common wrong entry 1423: Sentence 4 says the lottery 'avoids this', and the thing avoided, rewarding a feature with no bearing on the claim, is described only in sentence 2.
Common wrong entry 1234: Sentence 3 begins 'Its fairness', which must refer to the lottery; before sentence 4 no lottery has been mentioned.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 4 straight after sentence 1 because the lottery seems to answer the case directly, which leaves 'avoids this' without the problem it avoids.

Difficulty: Clear reference words ('either child', 'avoids this', 'Its fairness') fix the order once the opener is found. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C09-002 · Para jumble · MediumTITA
The five sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of five numbers as your answer.

1.Where those options are bad enough, where refusing would cost her something she cannot afford to lose, her 'yes' tells us more about her circumstances than about her wishes.

2.Consent is usually treated as a switch: either a person agreed to something or they did not.

3.The difference lies not in the words spoken, which may be identical, but in the options the person had when speaking them.

4.Yet an agreement given under heavy pressure, by a worker who will lose her job if she refuses extra hours, hardly carries the same moral weight as one freely offered.

5.Consent is therefore better understood as a matter of degree, its weight depending on how acceptable the alternatives to agreeing actually were.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 24315

Sentence 2 states the common all-or-nothing view of consent. Sentence 4 challenges it ('Yet') with the pressured worker. Sentence 3 explains 'The difference' between pressured and free agreement: it lies in the options available. Sentence 1 refers to 'those options' and applies the point to the worker, using 'her' from 4. Sentence 5 concludes ('therefore') that consent comes in degrees, which reverses the switch picture of 2.

Common wrong entry 24135: Sentence 1 speaks of 'those options', and options are introduced only in sentence 3 as what really separates pressured from free agreement.
Common wrong entry 23415: Sentence 3 explains 'The difference', which needs the contrast between pressured and freely offered agreement drawn in sentence 4.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 1 straight after sentence 4 because both describe the worker, which leaves 'those options' in sentence 1 without the options that sentence 3 introduces.

Difficulty: Order depends mainly on the logic of the idea: the example must be explained by the idea of options before the options can be judged and the switch picture replaced. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C09-003 · Para jumble · MediumTITA
The four sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3 and 4) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of four numbers as your answer.

1.The cost, however, is hidden rather than absent, because it falls on the meaning of praise itself.

2.White lies are usually defended as small kindnesses: telling a friend that a mediocre meal was delicious spares feelings and seems to cost nothing.

3.The friend who is always told that the meal was wonderful thus loses the one thing an honest friend could offer, which is a reason to believe it.

4.If everyone knows that compliments are given regardless of merit, then a compliment can no longer tell anyone that their work was actually good.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 2143

Sentence 2 states the usual defence of white lies, ending on the claim that they 'cost nothing'. Sentence 1 answers that claim: 'The cost, however, is hidden rather than absent' and locates it in 'the meaning of praise'. Sentence 4 explains that general mechanism: unconditional compliments carry no information. Sentence 3 returns to the friend and the meal and applies the mechanism ('thus'), so it must follow the general explanation in 4.

Common wrong entry 2134: Sentence 3 concludes with 'thus' that the friend has lost a reason to believe the praise, but that conclusion rests on the principle in sentence 4 that indiscriminate compliments carry no information.
Common wrong entry 2413: Sentence 4 explains how praise loses meaning, but the claim that the cost falls on praise is introduced only in sentence 1, which also answers 'cost nothing' in sentence 2.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 3 before sentence 4 because it mentions the friend and the meal from sentence 2, although its 'thus' depends on the principle that sentence 4 states.

Difficulty: The last two sentences are ordered by logic (principle before application) rather than by a pronoun. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C09-004 · Para jumble · DifficultTITA
The five sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of five numbers as your answer.

1.Repeat the step patiently enough, though, and the same principle commits you to calling a lone grain, or even nothing at all, a heap.

2.Denying the principle means insisting that somewhere there is an exact grain whose removal ends the heap, a boundary that no speaker could ever locate.

3.Keeping it instead means conceding that words like 'heap', 'tall' and 'bald' cannot be trusted in strict reasoning at all, which asks far more of them than they were made for.

4.Remove a single grain from a heap of sand and you still have a heap; no one seriously thinks that one grain could make that difference.

5.Something has to give, and the two obvious candidates each carry an unwelcome price.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 41523

Sentence 4 sets up the principle that one grain makes no difference. Sentence 1 shows that repeating the step leads to an absurd conclusion; 'the same principle' and 'the step' point back to 4. Only once the contradiction is in view can sentence 5 say 'Something has to give' and announce two candidates. Sentence 2 takes the first candidate, denying the principle, and sentence 3 the alternative, 'Keeping it instead', where 'it' is the principle and 'instead' requires the option already stated in 2.

Common wrong entry 41235: Sentence 5 could look like a summary, but 2 and 3 would then arrive with no announcement that there are two candidates, and 'Something has to give' after both responses makes no sense.
Common wrong entry 41532: Sentence 3 says 'Keeping it instead', which only makes sense once the opposite option, denying the principle, has been stated in sentence 2.

Common misconception: Treating sentence 5 as a closing summary after both options, or opening with it, without noticing that 'Something has to give' presupposes the contradiction reached in sentence 1.

Difficulty: Order depends mainly on the logic of the idea: the contradiction must be complete before 'Something has to give', and 'Keeping it instead' must answer the denial; the material is abstract and the two options look interchangeable at first glance. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-C09-005 · Para jumble · EasyTITA
The four sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3 and 4) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of four numbers as your answer.

1.Shoppers often say that they want as many options as possible, and shops compete with one another to provide them.

2.Yet a page listing dozens of nearly identical phone plans can leave a customer more hesitant than a page listing only a handful.

3.More choice, in other words, is welcome only up to the point where that effort and that risk of regret begin to outweigh its rewards.

4.The reason is that each extra option adds to the effort of comparing, and raises the chance of later regret about the plans not chosen.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 1243

Sentence 1 states the common belief that more options are better. Sentence 2 contradicts it with 'Yet' and the phone-plan example. Sentence 4 explains that observation ('The reason is'). Sentence 3 restates the conclusion in general terms ('in other words'), and its 'that effort and that risk of regret' points back to the reasons given in 4, so it must come last.

Common wrong entry 2143: Sentence 2 begins with 'Yet', which needs a prior claim to contrast with; that claim is the belief in sentence 1.
Common wrong entry 1234: Sentence 3 speaks of 'that effort and that risk of regret', which are named only in sentence 4, so sentence 3 cannot come before it.

Common misconception: Opening with sentence 2 because it contains the concrete example, although its 'Yet' needs the belief stated in sentence 1.

Difficulty: The order follows explicit connectives ('Yet', 'The reason is', 'in other words'). · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C09-006 · Para jumble · EasyTITA
The five sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of five numbers as your answer.

1.Advertisers exploit exactly this confusion when they repeat a brand name far more often than any new information would require.

2.One explanation is that familiar things are easier to take in, and this ease is mistaken for a sign that the thing itself is good.

3.A song that seemed dull on first hearing, for example, can become a favourite after it has been played often enough on the radio.

4.Repetition of this kind persuades, if it does, not by argument but by making the product feel like something we already know.

5.People often come to like things simply because they have encountered them many times before.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 53214

Sentence 5 states the general tendency. Sentence 3 illustrates it ('for example'). Sentence 2 offers 'One explanation', that ease of processing is mistaken for quality. Sentence 1 says advertisers exploit 'exactly this confusion', the mistake named in 2. Sentence 4 comments on 'Repetition of this kind', the advertisers' repetition of 1.

Common wrong entry 53124: Sentence 1 refers to 'exactly this confusion', which is the mistaking of ease for quality that sentence 2 describes.
Common wrong entry 52314: Sentence 3 illustrates sentence 5 with 'for example'; after sentence 2 it would interrupt the link between the explanation and 'this confusion' in sentence 1.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 1 right after sentence 3 because both give everyday examples, although 'this confusion' needs the explanation in sentence 2.

Difficulty: General claim, example, explanation, application and comment, each with a clear link. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C09-007 · Para jumble · MediumTITA
The four sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3 and 4) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of four numbers as your answer.

1.Each side adjusts away from that opening figure as talks proceed, but adjustments of this kind are usually too small.

2.In a negotiation over price, the first number mentioned tends to pull the final agreement towards itself, even when it was chosen with little justification.

3.Because the adjustment falls short, a seller who opens high can end up with a better price than one who opens with a reasonable figure and waits to be talked up.

4.For a buyer facing such a seller, the lesson is to name a figure first, or else to refuse to treat the seller's opening as the starting point of discussion.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 2134

Sentence 2 states the anchoring tendency. Sentence 1 explains it: adjustments from 'that opening figure' are too small. Sentence 3 draws the consequence for a seller who opens high, and its opening 'Because the adjustment falls short' refers to the insufficient adjustment described in 1. Sentence 4 draws the lesson for 'a buyer facing such a seller', that is, the high-opening seller of 3.

Common wrong entry 2314: Sentence 3 begins 'Because the adjustment falls short', but no adjustment has been mentioned until sentence 1; moving sentence 1 later also separates sentence 4 from 'such a seller'.
Common wrong entry 2143: Sentence 4 speaks of 'such a seller', the one who opens high, who is introduced only in sentence 3.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 3 before sentence 1 because it follows naturally from the first number pulling the deal, missing that its opening 'Because the adjustment falls short' rests on the mechanism stated in sentence 1.

Difficulty: Order depends mainly on the logic of the idea: the mechanism (too small adjustment) must come before the consequence for sellers and the lesson for buyers. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C09-008 · Para jumble · DifficultTITA
The four sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3 and 4) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of four numbers as your answer.

1.It struggles, however, with the traveller who knows perfectly well that flying is safer than driving and is terrified on every flight all the same.

2.On one influential view, an emotion is a kind of judgement: to be angry, for instance, is to judge that one has been wronged.

3.The view neatly explains why anger usually fades once we learn that an apparent insult was an accident, since the judgement on which it rested has been withdrawn.

4.If her fear were simply a judgement of danger, her knowledge should dissolve it, and its persistence suggests that emotions answer partly to something older and less easily corrected than belief.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 2314

Sentence 2 states the view that emotions are judgements. Sentence 3 gives its success: anger fades when the judgement is withdrawn. Sentence 1 turns ('however') to a case the view handles badly, and its 'however' needs the success in 3. Sentence 4 analyses 'her fear', the traveller's from 1, and draws the conclusion that emotions are not only judgements.

Common wrong entry 2134: Sentence 1 can follow the view directly, but sentence 4 then comes after the anger example, and 'her fear' must sit next to the traveller it refers to.
Common wrong entry 2341: Sentence 4 speaks of 'her fear' and 'her knowledge', which need the traveller of sentence 1 to be introduced first.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 1 right after sentence 2 because its 'It struggles' can seem to answer the view directly, which separates the traveller from 'her fear' in sentence 4 and leaves 'however' with no success to qualify.

Difficulty: Order depends mainly on the logic of the idea: success of a theory, its counterexample and the lesson drawn from the counterexample must come in that sequence; the content is abstract and the counterexample can look like it belongs anywhere after the view. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-C09-009 · Para jumble · EasyTITA
The four sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3 and 4) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of four numbers as your answer.

1.When people from these two traditions talk, each may misjudge the other, one as evasive and the other as rash, without either intending any offence.

2.How long a pause in conversation can last before it feels awkward varies a great deal from one culture to another.

3.Elsewhere the same silence is read as disagreement or discomfort, and speakers hurry to fill it with words.

4.In some places a silence of several seconds is a sign of respect, showing that the listener is giving thought to what was said.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 2431

Sentence 2 states that the tolerance for pauses varies across cultures. Sentence 4 gives one case ('In some places'). Sentence 3 gives the contrasting case ('Elsewhere the same silence'). Sentence 1 refers to 'these two traditions' and describes the misunderstanding between them.

Common wrong entry 4321: Sentence 2 states the general claim that sentences 4 and 3 illustrate; placed after them it would interrupt the move to 'these two traditions' in sentence 1.

Common misconception: Opening with sentence 4 because it is concrete, although the general claim in sentence 2 is what the two contrasting cases illustrate.

Difficulty: General claim, two contrasting cases and the consequence, each signposted. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C09-010 · Para jumble · MediumTITA
The four sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3 and 4) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of four numbers as your answer.

1.Their fear was that publishers chasing mass sales would print mainly sensational fiction and crowd out serious work.

2.When cheap paperback editions first became widely available, some critics warned that they would lower the standard of literature along with the price of books.

3.What actually happened was more mixed: sensational fiction did multiply, but classics and serious essays also reached readers who had never been able to afford hardbound copies.

4.The episode suggests that lowering the cost of access tends to enlarge every kind of reading at once, rather than simply replacing the better kind with the worse.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 2134

Sentence 2 introduces the critics' warning about cheap paperbacks. Sentence 1 spells out 'Their fear'. Sentence 3 reports 'What actually happened', which answers the fear. Sentence 4 draws the general lesson from 'The episode'.

Common wrong entry 2143: Sentence 4 draws a lesson that contradicts the critics, but the evidence for it, the mixed outcome, appears only in sentence 3.
Common wrong entry 2314: Sentence 3 answers a specific fear, crowding out by sensational fiction, which is stated only in sentence 1.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 4 before sentence 3 as if the general lesson came first, although 'The episode suggests' depends on the outcome reported in sentence 3.

Difficulty: The expectation and outcome pair must be read for sense: the lesson in the last sentence follows from the mixed outcome, not from the fear. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C09-011 · Para jumble · MediumTITA
The five sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of five numbers as your answer.

1.Each such decision is reasonable on its own terms, which is precisely what makes the overall loss so hard to reverse.

2.More commonly they fade within a few generations, as parents decide that their children's prospects lie in the language of schools and employers.

3.Campaigns that appeal only to heritage and pride ask families to set aside the very calculation that led them to switch in the first place.

4.Efforts that last tend instead to give the language practical uses, in local media, administration or trade, so that speaking it no longer feels like a sacrifice.

5.Minority languages are lost far less often through outright prohibition than one might suppose.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 52134

Sentence 5 sets aside prohibition as the usual cause. Sentence 2 gives the more common cause, parents' decisions. Sentence 1 comments on 'Each such decision': being reasonable, the decisions are hard to reverse. Sentence 3 explains why appeals to pride fail against that reasonable calculation. Sentence 4 offers the alternative that works ('instead').

Common wrong entry 52314: Sentence 1 says 'Each such decision', which must sit next to the parents' decisions in sentence 2; after sentence 3 it would refer to campaigns.
Common wrong entry 52143: Sentence 4 describes efforts that work 'instead', a contrast with the failing campaigns that sentence 3 must first describe.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 3 right after sentence 2, since both mention families' choices, which separates 'Each such decision' in sentence 1 from the decisions it refers to.

Difficulty: Order depends mainly on the logic of the idea: the reasonableness of each family's choice must be established before the paragraph can explain why appeals to pride fail and what works instead. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C09-012 · Para jumble · MediumTITA
The four sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3 and 4) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of four numbers as your answer.

1.Yet the director still decides who is promoted and who is let go, so the equality being celebrated extends only to the way people speak to one another.

2.Many workplaces have abandoned titles and surnames, so that a junior clerk now addresses the managing director by her first name.

3.What has disappeared is the outward marker of rank rather than rank itself, which may make power harder to see and therefore harder to question.

4.The change is often celebrated as a flattening of hierarchy, a sign that everyone is now treated as an equal.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 2413

Sentence 2 describes the shift to first names. Sentence 4 reports how 'The change' is celebrated, as making everyone equal. Sentence 1 answers with 'Yet': the director still holds power, so 'the equality being celebrated', the equality claimed in 4, is only verbal. Sentence 3 states the conclusion about outward markers and real rank.

Common wrong entry 2143: Sentence 1 limits 'the equality being celebrated', and the celebration is reported only in sentence 4; placed before it, the rebuttal would precede the view it rebuts.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 1 right after sentence 2, since 'Yet the director still decides' seems to answer the first-name example directly, which leaves 'the equality being celebrated' without the celebration it refers to.

Difficulty: The middle pair is ordered by sense: the rebuttal must follow the celebration it rebuts, and both sentences can look like the second. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C09-013 · Para jumble · DifficultTITA
The five sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of five numbers as your answer.

1.Oddly, repaying it at once, with something of exactly equal value, can give offence as well.

2.A gift appears to be the one transaction in which nothing at all is expected in return.

3.An immediate and precise return turns the exchange into a purchase, cancelling the debt and with it the bond.

4.What the gift creates, then, is neither a demand for payment nor a freedom from all obligation, but a lasting and slightly indefinite debt, and it is the indefiniteness that keeps the relationship alive.

5.Yet in many societies a gift that is never reciprocated slowly sours the relationship it was meant to strengthen.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 25134

Sentence 2 states the appearance that gifts expect nothing. Sentence 5 contradicts it ('Yet'): an unreturned gift sours the relationship. Sentence 1 adds the opposite failure, and its 'as well' requires the first kind of offence from 5. Sentence 3 explains why exact immediate repayment offends, so it must follow 1. Sentence 4 combines both failures ('then') into the conclusion that the gift creates an indefinite debt.

Common wrong entry 21534: Sentence 1 says exact repayment can give offence 'as well', so the first offence, the unreturned gift of sentence 5, must come before it; sentence 3 would also be cut off from the repayment it explains.
Common wrong entry 25314: Sentence 3 explains why an immediate and precise return offends, which assumes that sentence 1 has already raised the case of repaying at once.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 1 before sentence 5 because 'Oddly' seems to answer the opening claim directly, missing that 'as well' needs an earlier offence.

Difficulty: Order depends mainly on the logic of the idea: two opposite failures must both be in place before the conclusion, and the 'as well' that orders them is easy to overlook. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-C09-014 · Para jumble · EasyTITA
The four sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3 and 4) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of four numbers as your answer.

1.For a time, canals were the cheapest way of moving heavy goods such as coal and stone across parts of Europe.

2.Many of those that survive are now used mainly for leisure, a quiet afterlife for what was once vital industrial transport.

3.Once railways spread, however, they offered speed as well as capacity, and most canals steadily lost their traffic.

4.A single horse walking along the towpath could pull a barge carrying far more than any cart on the roads of the day.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 1432

Sentence 1 introduces canals as the cheapest freight route. Sentence 4 explains why, with the horse and barge. Sentence 3 turns ('however') to the railways that took their traffic. Sentence 2 describes 'those that survive', which presupposes the decline in 3.

Common wrong entry 1342: Sentence 4 supports the cheapness claimed in sentence 1; after the railways in sentence 3 it would break the link between the decline and the canals 'that survive' in sentence 2.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 3 before sentence 4, although the horse and barge explain the cheapness claimed in sentence 1 and belong before the decline.

Difficulty: Chronology and a clear 'however' mark the order. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C09-015 · Para jumble · MediumTITA
The four sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3 and 4) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of four numbers as your answer.

1.Over time, however, the repetition of the same forms across thousands of copies gave readers a sense that one spelling was correct.

2.Before printing, a single English word might be spelled in several ways, sometimes by the same scribe on the same page.

3.Printing did not abolish this variety at once, since early printers sometimes adjusted spellings simply to fit a line of type.

4.The standard that dictionaries later recorded was thus less a deliberate reform than the slow sediment of habits formed in the print shop.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 2314

Sentence 2 describes spelling variety before printing. Sentence 3 says printing 'did not abolish this variety at once', referring back to 2. Sentence 1 turns ('Over time, however') to the gradual effect of repetition. Sentence 4 concludes ('thus') that the standard grew out of print habits.

Common wrong entry 2134: Sentence 1 opens 'Over time, however', which reverses the claim in sentence 3 that printing did not end variety at once; directly after sentence 2 there is nothing to reverse.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 1 directly after sentence 2 because it describes the effect of printing, which leaves 'however' with no immediate claim to qualify.

Difficulty: Chronology plus a concession and its reversal; the 'however' in the third sentence must answer the second. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C09-016 · Para jumble · MediumTITA
The five sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of five numbers as your answer.

1.Such evasion, like the fear that prompted it, suggests that the census is less a neutral act of description than an instrument through which a state extends its reach.

2.A government that wants to tax, conscript or feed its population must first know who that population is and where it lives.

3.Their fear was well founded, since being counted made a household visible, and visibility was the precondition of being taxed or summoned.

4.Early censuses were undertaken for exactly these purposes, and people often had good reason to fear the officials who came to count them.

5.It is hardly surprising, then, that people wishing to stay beyond a state's reach have sometimes avoided being counted, giving false numbers or simply being away when the officials came.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 24351

Sentence 2 states what a government needs to know in order to tax, conscript or feed people. Sentence 4 says early censuses served 'exactly these purposes', the three named in 2, and that people feared the counters. Sentence 3 explains 'Their fear', which needs 4. Sentence 5 draws the consequence ('then') that people avoided being counted. Sentence 1 refers to 'Such evasion', the avoidance described in 5, and draws the general reinterpretation of the census.

Common wrong entry 24531: Sentence 3 begins 'Their fear', which must follow the fear of the officials mentioned in sentence 4; after sentence 5 it would refer to people avoiding the count, and the 'then' of sentence 5 would lack its reason.
Common wrong entry 23451: Sentence 3 explains 'Their fear', and no one has been said to fear anything until sentence 4.
Common wrong entry 24315: Sentence 1 begins 'Such evasion', and the evasion, people avoiding the count or giving false numbers, is described only in sentence 5.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 5 straight after sentence 4 because both describe people's response to the counters, which separates 'Their fear' in sentence 3 from the fear it refers to.

Difficulty: Order depends mainly on the logic of the idea: purpose, fear, the reason the fear was justified, the response and the reinterpretation form a causal chain. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C09-017 · Para jumble · DifficultTITA
The four sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3 and 4) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of four numbers as your answer.

1.Complaints that manners, learning or public life have declined since some better age can be found in the writings of a great many periods.

2.What such evidence shows, most likely, is a habit of comparing an untidy present with a past known only through what was worth remembering, a contest the past always wins.

3.Since that conclusion is absurd, the complaints are better read as evidence about those who make them than about the times they describe.

4.If each generation's complaint were accurate, the decline would have been continuous for thousands of years, leaving the present unimaginably worse than any past.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 1432

Sentence 1 observes that complaints of decline recur across many periods. Sentence 4 tests them: if all were accurate, decline would be continuous and the present unimaginably bad. Sentence 3 rejects 'that conclusion' as absurd and reinterprets the complaints as 'evidence about those who make them'. Sentence 2 says what 'such evidence' shows about the complainers, so it must follow 3.

Common wrong entry 1243: Sentence 2 can look like an explanation of the complaints in sentence 1, but it speaks of 'such evidence', and the complaints become evidence about their makers only in sentence 3.
Common wrong entry 2143: Sentence 2 has a general ring that tempts as an opener, but 'such evidence' needs a prior mention of evidence, which only sentence 3 supplies.

Common misconception: Moving sentence 2 forward as an explanation of the complaints in sentence 1, missing that 'such evidence' refers to the reinterpretation reached only in sentence 3.

Difficulty: Order depends mainly on the logic of the idea: a reduction to absurdity has to be run before the complaints can be reinterpreted, and the explanatory sentence tempts as an early or opening sentence. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-C09-018 · Para jumble · EasyTITA
The five sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of five numbers as your answer.

1.Many prices in shops end in nine, so that an item is marked at 499 rupees rather than at a round 500.

2.One explanation is that shoppers read prices from left to right and give most weight to the first digit they see.

3.Some upmarket shops deliberately avoid such endings for the same reason, preferring round figures that suggest quality rather than a bargain.

4.A price beginning with four then feels distinctly cheaper than one beginning with five, even though the gap is almost nothing.

5.The difference of a single rupee is trivial, yet sellers persist with the practice in almost every kind of shop.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 15243

Sentence 1 introduces prices ending in nine. Sentence 5 notes the puzzle: 'The difference of a single rupee' is trivial yet the practice persists. Sentence 2 offers 'One explanation'. Common wrong entry 15324: Sentence 3 says upmarket shops avoid such endings 'for the same reason', which has not been given until sentences 2 and 4.
Common wrong entry 12543: Sentence 2 offers 'One explanation', which needs the puzzle of the trivial rupee in sentence 5 to explain.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 3 right after sentence 5 as a second observation about pricing, although 'for the same reason' needs the explanation in sentences 2 and 4.

Difficulty: Observation, puzzle, explanation, consequence and a contrasting case, each with a clear link. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 2134

Sentence 2 states the readers' complaint. Sentence 1 identifies the assumption behind 'The complaint'. Sentence 3 challenges 'That assumption': a film cannot contain a whole novel. Sentence 4 proposes 'The fairer test' as the conclusion ('therefore').

Common wrong entry 2314: Sentence 3 begins 'That assumption', and the only assumption in the paragraph is the one stated in sentence 1; sentence 1's 'The complaint' must also directly follow sentence 2.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 3 before sentence 1, although 'That assumption' refers to the belief, stated in sentence 1, that an adaptation should include as much as possible.

Difficulty: Clear chain of complaint, assumption, objection and conclusion, marked by 'The complaint', 'That assumption' and 'therefore'. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

Answer: 1324

Difficulty: The order follows the argument: rule, objection, the rival rule that answers the objection, then the compromise. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

Common misconception: Opening with sentence 1 because it seems to introduce salt, though 'it' needs the subject named in sentence 2 and the reasons need a claim to support.

Difficulty: The paradox at the end (resented because efficient) only works if the reasons for efficiency come before their turn into a grievance. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

3.On this account, owner and buyer are not really pricing the same thing, since the owner is weighing the loss of a mug and the buyer the gain of one.

4.The gap is puzzling on a simple view of value, since a few minutes of ownership cannot have made the mug any more useful.

5.Their figures differ, then, because they are answering different questions, and neither need be mistaken about the mug itself.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 24135

Sentence 2 describes the gap between what owners ask and buyers offer. Sentence 4 calls 'The gap' puzzling. Sentence 1 offers 'One explanation', loss weighing more than gain. Sentence 3 applies it ('On this account'): owner and buyer value different things. Sentence 5 concludes ('then') that 'Their figures' differ because they answer different questions; 'Their' needs the owner and buyer named in 3, and the 'different questions' are the loss and the gain that 3 distinguishes.

Common wrong entry 24153: Sentence 5 says the parties are 'answering different questions', which follows from sentence 3, where they are shown to be weighing a loss and a gain; sentence 1 states only the general asymmetry, and 'Their figures' needs the owner and buyer of 3.
Common wrong entry 21435: Sentence 1 offers 'One explanation', which needs a puzzle to explain; the gap is called puzzling only in sentence 4.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 5 before sentence 3 as a direct consequence of loss aversion, although the claim that the two answer different questions depends on the point in sentence 3 that they are weighing different prospects.

Difficulty: Order depends mainly on the logic of the idea: the conclusion that the two parties answer different questions follows only once they are shown to be weighing a loss and a gain, so the last pair is fixed mainly by reasoning. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

1.As cheaper copies spread downward, the style loses its power to mark distinction, and the wealthy abandon it for something new.

2.The theory thus accounts neatly for why fashion never stands still: every act of imitation from below sets off a fresh flight from above.

3.Even these reversals may confirm the underlying logic, however, since what the affluent borrow from below is often prized because it signals a knowingness that money alone cannot buy.

4.An old explanation of fashion holds that styles begin among the wealthy, who adopt them precisely because others cannot afford to.

5.Yet the theory's neatness is also its weakness, for in recent decades many styles have plainly travelled the other way, from street clothing and working dress into expensive boutiques.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 41253

Sentence 4 states the theory. Sentence 1 continues the mechanism with 'cheaper copies' of 'the style'. Sentence 2 sums up what the theory explains ('thus accounts neatly'). Sentence 5 turns on that 'neatness' and raises the counter-example of styles travelling 'the other way'. Sentence 3 answers with 'these reversals', arguing they may still fit the logic of distinction.

Common wrong entry 41532: Sentence 5 speaks of 'the theory's neatness', which is the judgement made in sentence 2; and sentence 2 restates the theory as if unchallenged, so it cannot come after the reversals have been raised.
Common wrong entry 42153: Sentence 2 says 'thus', summing up a mechanism of imitation and flight that is completed only in sentence 1.

Common misconception: Ending with sentence 2 because it sounds like a summary, which leaves 'the theory's neatness' in sentence 5 referring to a judgement not yet made.

Difficulty: Five sentences with a claim, objection and reply; the summary sentence is tempting as a closer but must precede the objection. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C09-181 · Para jumble · DifficultTITA
The five sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of five numbers as your answer.

1.Farmers must decide how much to plant months before they learn what the harvest will fetch, and many simply assume that next season's price will match this season's.

2.After a year of high prices, that assumption leads many of them to plant more, and the larger harvest that follows pushes the price down.

3.If that diagnosis is right, the remedy may lie less in subsidies than in information, since farmers who could see how much was being planted in total would stop treating today's price as a forecast.

4.What sustains the swing is neither the weather nor any change in tastes but the lag between decision and reward, together with the habit of reading the future off the present.

5.Expecting the low price to last, they plant less the next season, and the smaller harvest sends the price back up, so that the swing begins again.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 12543

Sentence 1 sets up the lag and the farmers' assumption that next season's price will match this one. Sentence 2 applies 'that assumption' to a year of high prices: more planting, a larger harvest, a falling price. Sentence 5 can only come next, because 'the low price' it expects to last is the fall produced in 2, and it completes the cycle. Sentence 4 explains what sustains 'the swing' once the swing has been described in full. Sentence 3 draws a remedy 'If that diagnosis is right', which needs the diagnosis of 4 before it.

Common wrong entry 15243: Sentence 5 begins 'Expecting the low price to last', but no low price exists until the larger harvest of sentence 2 has pushed the price down; and 'that assumption' in 2 would then be separated from the assumption stated in 1.
Common wrong entry 12534: Sentence 3 proposes a remedy 'If that diagnosis is right', and the diagnosis, the lag and the habit of reading the future off the present, is given only in sentence 4.

Common misconception: Placing the remedy in sentence 3 straight after the description of the cycle, as if the cycle alone suggested it, which leaves 'that diagnosis' with nothing to refer to and puts the explanation after its consequence.

Difficulty: The two phases of the cycle look interchangeable until the reader sees that 'the low price' must be the fall caused by overplanting; the diagnosis and remedy then follow the logic of explanation before consequence. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-C09-183 · Para jumble · DifficultTITA

1.A long-standing principle in ethics holds that it can be permissible to cause a harm one foresees but does not intend, when it would be wrong to cause the same harm as a goal or a means.

2.This objection loses much of its force once intention is tested not by how an act is described but by whether the agent's plan would still succeed if, by some fluke, the harm did not occur.

3.Critics object that the line between intending and merely foreseeing is too easily redrawn, since almost any harmful act can be redescribed so that the harm appears incidental.

4.By that measure, an engineer who opens a sluice to save a town, foreseeing that fields downstream will flood, succeeds even if the fields happen to stay dry; one who floods them to drive the farmers away does not.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 1324

Sentence 1 states the principle distinguishing foreseen from intended harm. Sentence 3 gives the critics' objection that the line is easily redrawn. Sentence 2 answers 'This objection' with a test: would the plan still succeed if the harm did not occur? Sentence 4 applies 'that measure' to the two engineers: one 'succeeds even if the fields happen to stay dry', the other does not. The example is not a mere illustration of the principle; its point (success even if the fields stay dry) is the counterfactual test, so it must follow sentence 2.

Common wrong entry 1432: The example in sentence 4 judges success by whether the plan works 'even if the fields happen to stay dry', which is the test introduced in sentence 2; 'By that measure' has nothing to refer to straight after sentence 1.
Common wrong entry 1234: Sentence 2 answers 'This objection', which is first stated in sentence 3.

Common misconception: Placing the engineer example in sentence 4 right after sentence 1 as a natural illustration of the principle, missing that 'By that measure' and the 'even if the fields happen to stay dry' clause apply the test given only in sentence 2.

Difficulty: The tempting order puts the example after the principle; rejecting it needs the logic of the idea, seeing that the example applies the counterfactual test, not the bare principle. · Exams: CAT, XAT

1.Once the choice is made, however, their ratings tend to drift apart, with the chosen option rising in esteem and the rejected one falling.

2.Before choosing between two attractive options, people often rate them as nearly equal in appeal.

3.If this is right, the satisfaction people report with their choices partly reflects the choosing itself, not only the merits of what they chose.

4.A common explanation is that holding a decision while still valuing what was given up is uncomfortable, and revising one's opinions is the easiest way to ease that discomfort.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 2143

Sentence 2 gives the ratings before a choice. Sentence 1 contrasts, with 'however', the ratings after it. Sentence 4 offers 'A common explanation' of that drift. Sentence 3 draws an implication 'If this is right', which needs the explanation first. The finding, its explanation and its implication must come in that order.

Common wrong entry 2134: Sentence 3 is conditional on an explanation ('If this is right'), and the explanation is supplied only in sentence 4.
Common wrong entry 1243: Sentence 1 says 'Once the choice is made, however', contrasting with the equal ratings before the choice in sentence 2.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 3 before sentence 4, since both interpret the finding, which leaves 'If this is right' with no explanation to be right.

Difficulty: Order follows the logic of the idea: observation, explanation, then an implication that is conditional on the explanation. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

4.To a concert audience the conductor can seem the least necessary person on stage, the only one who makes no sound.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 4312

Sentence 4 states how the conductor appears to an audience. Sentence 3 answers with 'Yet', explaining the timing problem of a large orchestra. Sentence 1 shows that the beat solves that problem, 'them' being the players of 3. Sentence 2 adds, with 'moreover', a second contribution, so it follows the first.

Common wrong entry 4321: Sentence 1 solves the timing problem of sentence 3 and its 'them' refers to the players there; placing sentence 2 between them breaks the link and leaves 'moreover' adding to a problem rather than a contribution.

Common misconception: Ending with sentence 1 after sentence 2, which separates the solution from the timing problem it solves.

Difficulty: Problem and solution must stay adjacent; the additive 'moreover' must follow a first contribution. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

Answer and explanation

Sentence 1 raises the expectation that 'such losses' would make people wary and answers it: the loss is 'felt by no one in particular'. Difficulty: The second sentence states the retailer's interest in a compact phrase that only the third unpacks, and the close runs from an objection to its answer to the consequence, so the order rests on the logic of invisible loss rather than on pronouns. · Exams: CAT, XAT

2.Through all these changes the fan's loyalty often survives, attaching itself to the name and colours rather than to any particular people.

3.Few attachments are as durable as the one a person forms with a sports club in childhood.

4.In this sense supporters are loyal less to a team than to a story about themselves that the team allows them to keep telling.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 3124

Sentence 3 introduces the subject, the durability of a childhood attachment to a club. Sentence 1 lists the changes a club undergoes. Sentence 2 refers to 'all these changes' and says loyalty survives them. Sentence 4 begins 'In this sense' and interprets that survival as loyalty to a story about oneself.

Common wrong entry 1324: Sentence 1 cannot open: 'the team' and 'its ground' assume a club that only sentence 3 introduces, and 'all these changes' in sentence 2 must follow the list directly.

Common misconception: Starting with sentence 1 because it is vivid, although it never names what is changing until sentence 3 has introduced the club.

Difficulty: Reference phrases ('all these changes', 'In this sense') fix the order once the opener is found. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C09-253 · Para jumble · DifficultTITA
The five sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of five numbers as your answer.

1.A name picked for its distinctiveness thus becomes common, and in becoming common it begins to sound dated to the next cohort of parents, who avoid it for the very reason it was once chosen.

2.Because parents of the same generation have heard much the same names while growing up, their ears agree, and they arrive independently at the same few choices.

3.No industry promotes given names, and nobody profits when a particular one becomes popular.

4.The puzzle dissolves once we notice how parents tend to choose: they look for a name that is uncommon but not strange, one that sounds fresh to their ear.

5.Yet the popularity of a name rises and falls on the familiar curve of a fad, climbing slowly, peaking and fading within a generation, as though someone were marketing it.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 35421

Sentence 3 states the background fact that nobody markets names. Sentence 5 sets up the puzzle with 'Yet': names still behave like marketed fads. Sentence 4 calls this 'the puzzle' and begins the answer with the way parents choose, seeking freshness. Sentence 2 adds why many parents converge on the same names. Sentence 1 concludes with 'thus': the name chosen as distinctive becomes common and then dated, which completes the rise and the fall described in sentence 5.

Common wrong entry 35142: Sentence 1 repeats the rise and fall of sentence 5 but its 'thus' and 'distinctiveness' depend on the preference for freshness in sentence 4 and the convergence in sentence 2, so it cannot follow sentence 5 directly.
Common wrong entry 35241: Sentence 2 says the parents' 'ears agree', but what their ears are listening for, a fresh but not strange name, is introduced only in sentence 4, which also names 'the puzzle' and so must come right after sentence 5.
Common wrong entry 35412: After sentence 4 the 'thus' of sentence 1 has no ground, because a preference for fresh names does not make any one name common until sentence 2 shows that parents converge on the same choices; sentence 2 placed last would explain a result already narrated.

Common misconception: Putting sentence 1 immediately after sentence 5 because both describe a name rising and falling, which leaves 'distinctiveness' and 'thus' without the reasoning of sentences 4 and 2.

Difficulty: Five abstract sentences; the closing sentence echoes the fad curve and tempts an early placement, and the two explanatory sentences must be ordered by seeing that convergence presupposes the preference for freshness. · Exams: CAT, XAT

2.Privacy, on this view, is not the enemy of collaboration but one of its conditions, because people share half-formed ideas only where they can afford to be wrong in front of a few listeners.

3.In many offices that adopted them, however, the effect has often seemed to be the reverse, as people put on headphones and send messages to colleagues a few desks away.

CAT-VARC-C09-257 · Para jumble · DifficultTITA
The four sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3 and 4) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of four numbers as your answer.

1.The earliest surviving tablets from Mesopotamia, however, consist overwhelmingly of lists of goods, rations and debts kept by administrators.

2.If so, writing began not as a way of capturing language but as a way of keeping count, and only gradually acquired the power to say whatever a speaker could.

3.It is tempting to suppose that writing was invented to record what people wished to say: their stories, their laws and their prayers.

4.On one influential account, these marks descend from small clay tokens that stood for quantities of grain or livestock long before anyone thought of representing a spoken word.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 3142

Sentence 3 states a natural assumption about why writing was invented. Sentence 1 challenges it with 'however': the earliest tablets are administrative lists. Sentence 4 traces 'these marks' to accounting tokens. Sentence 2 opens with 'If so', accepting the account in sentence 4, and concludes that writing began as counting. The conclusion contradicts the assumption in sentence 3, so sentence 3 must come first as the view being overturned, not later as a premise.

Common wrong entry 1432: Ending the evidence with sentence 3 turns the assumption into a premise, so 'If so' in sentence 2 would accept the very view the evidence has just undermined.
Common wrong entry 3214: Sentence 2 concludes that writing began as counting, which does not follow from sentence 3; 'If so' must accept the token account in sentence 4.

Common misconception: Reading 'If so' in sentence 2 as following sentence 3, which would make the passage conclude that writing began as counting from a premise that says the opposite.

Difficulty: Solvers must see that the opening assumption is the view under attack and that 'If so' accepts only the token account; an order that moves the assumption later looks fluent but reverses the argument. · Exams: CAT, XAT

2.When states began to require military service from the whole adult male population, they were asking for something far greater than money.

3.Such demands for a voice shaped political debate in several countries, where the extension of the vote was argued for, at least in part, as the fair return for a burden every citizen now shared.

4.Men compelled to risk death for a nation found it natural to ask why they should have no voice in how that nation was governed.

3.The choice between the two forms of pay therefore turns less on how hard people are willing to work than on what the employer is able to measure.

1.Lending to small groups whose members are jointly liable for one another's debts addresses both problems without any new information ever reaching the lender.

2.By choosing and pressing their partners in this way, the members in effect do the screening and the collecting that the lender cannot, using knowledge no loan officer could gather cheaply.

3.A lender advancing small sums to poor households faces two problems at once: it cannot tell careful borrowers from careless ones, and it has nothing to seize when a loan goes bad.

4.A member who must answer for a partner's default will choose partners known to be reliable, and will press any partner who begins to fall behind with repayments.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 3142

Sentence 3 sets out the lender's two problems: it cannot sort borrowers, and it cannot recover bad loans. Sentence 1 proposes joint-liability group lending as a remedy for both. Sentence 4 says what a member who must 'answer for a partner's default', that is, a member of such a group, will do: choose reliable partners and press laggards. Sentence 2 sums up ('in this way', 'in effect'): choosing partners is the screening and pressing laggards is the collecting, the two tasks that answer the two problems of sentence 3.

Common wrong entry 3124: Sentence 2 begins 'By choosing and pressing their partners in this way', which refers to the behaviour described in sentence 4; placed before it, the summary would sum up something not yet said.
Common wrong entry 3412: Sentence 4 speaks of a member who must 'answer for a partner's default', which presupposes the jointly liable groups introduced only in sentence 1.

Common misconception: Ending with sentence 4 because it describes the mechanism in detail, although sentence 2 is a summary that maps the mechanism onto the two problems and cannot precede what it summarises.

Difficulty: The lender's two problems must be matched with 'screening' and 'collecting' in the summary; the summary and the mechanism are easily swapped. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-C09-270 · Para jumble · DifficultTITA
The five sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of five numbers as your answer.

1.When two such objects collide, they do not simply vanish but shatter into thousands of fragments travelling at enormous speeds.

2.Every satellite launched into low orbit adds to a population of objects that will remain there, in many cases, for years or decades.

3.A fragment no larger than a coin can disable a satellite, and a disabled satellite struck again becomes a fresh source of fragments.

4.Because of this tipping point, the danger does not grow in step with the number of objects in orbit, which is why a modest rise in launches may matter far more than it seems.

5.Beyond a certain density, then, collisions could feed on themselves, multiplying debris faster than it falls back to Earth.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 21354

Sentence 2 establishes the growing population of orbiting objects. Sentence 1 says collisions between 'such objects' create fragments. Sentence 3 says fragments disable satellites that then produce more fragments. Sentence 5 draws the first inference ('then'): past a certain density the process becomes self-sustaining. Sentence 4 draws the second inference, 'Because of this tipping point', which refers to the density threshold of sentence 5: because there is such a threshold, danger does not rise in proportion to numbers.

Common wrong entry 21345: Sentence 4 says danger does not grow in step with numbers, which follows from the existence of a runaway threshold; that threshold is introduced only in sentence 5, so sentence 4 cannot precede it.
Common wrong entry 23154: Sentence 3 speaks of fragments before any collision has produced them; the fragments come from the collision described in sentence 1.

Common misconception: Swapping the last two sentences because both are conclusions, although the claim that danger is not proportional to numbers depends on the threshold described in sentence 5.

Difficulty: Two successive conclusions must be ordered by which one depends on the other; both carry inference words. · Exams: CAT, XAT

4.But two patients who both say 'seven' may mean very different things, since each anchors the scale to the worst pain they have personally known.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 3241

Sentence 3 introduces the pain scale. Sentence 2 gives its appeal: 'The number' is easy to record and compare. Sentence 4 objects with 'But': the same number can mean different things to different patients. Sentence 1 concludes ('therefore') where the score is useful, which follows from the objection in 4 and limits the claim about comparison in 2.

Common wrong entry 3421: Sentence 4 objects to comparing patients, and that only makes sense once sentence 2 has claimed the number is easy to compare; placed after 4, sentence 2 also breaks the 'therefore' link to sentence 1.

Common misconception: Putting sentence 4 before sentence 2, which makes 'But' contrast with the bare description of the scale instead of with its supposed comparability.

Difficulty: Order depends mainly on the logic of the idea: the conclusion narrows the use of the score, which needs both the claimed comparability and the objection to it. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

3.This persistence can be useful, since a problem left open overnight is sometimes solved in the morning without further effort.

4.The interrupted task, whether a half-written letter or an unsolved puzzle, keeps returning to awareness, as though the mind were reluctant to let go of an unreached goal.

5.The burden, interestingly, often lifts once a person writes down a concrete plan for completing the task, even before any of the work is done.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 24315

Sentence 2 states the general observation. Sentence 4 picks up 'The interrupted task' and illustrates it. Sentence 3 calls this 'This persistence' and gives its benefit. Sentence 1 adds 'also' a cost, which needs the benefit before it. Sentence 5 says how 'The burden' of sentence 1 lifts, so it comes last.

Common wrong entry 24135: Sentence 1 says the persistence 'can also be a burden', so it must follow the first effect given in sentence 3.
Common wrong entry 24531: Sentence 5 explains how 'The burden' lifts, but no burden has been mentioned until sentence 1.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 5 after sentence 4 because both describe how the mind holds on to a task, which leaves 'The burden' without a referent.

Difficulty: Five sentences with a benefit and cost pair and a final remedy; students must track 'This persistence', 'also' and 'The burden' across the chain. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

Sentence 3 describes what monitors are designed to do. Sentence 2 says that cautious limits make most alarms false. Sentence 1 describes how staff respond to 'dozens of false alarms'. Sentence 5 names 'The result', the irony that the system hides real emergencies. Sentence 4 draws the conclusion with 'therefore' that fewer alarms can be safer, which follows only once the irony has been stated.

Common wrong entry 32145: Sentence 4 concludes 'therefore' that reducing alarms can make a ward safer; that rests on the irony in sentence 5, and 'The result' in sentence 5 must follow the staff behaviour of sentence 1 directly.
Common wrong entry 31254: Sentence 1 speaks of 'false alarms', but only sentence 2 explains that most alarms are false, so sentence 1 cannot come before it.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 4 before sentence 5 because both comment on the problem, although the claim that fewer alarms can be safer depends on the irony spelled out in sentence 5.

Difficulty: Order depends mainly on the causal logic: design, false alarms, staff response, ironic result, remedy. · Exams: CAT, XAT, NMAT, SNAP, CMAT, MAH-CET

CAT-VARC-C09-344 · Para jumble · DifficultTITA
The four sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3 and 4) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of four numbers as your answer.

1.Ecologists who have revisited such cases are wary of precisely this tidiness, pointing out that drought, hunting and patterns of land use often changed during the same years.

2.Its appeal lies partly in its moral shape: a wrong is undone, and a damaged system rights itself once a single missing piece is restored.

3.Few conservation stories are told with more enthusiasm than that of the returning predator who, by frightening grazing animals, allows forests and riverbanks to recover.

4.None of this shows that the predators made no difference; it shows that a before-and-after comparison cannot by itself say how much.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 3214

Sentence 3 introduces the popular story of the returning predator. Sentence 2 explains 'Its appeal', the story's moral shape of a single missing piece restored. Sentence 1 reports that ecologists are wary of 'precisely this tidiness', which refers to that moral shape, and supplies the rival causes. Sentence 4 qualifies the scepticism: 'None of this' shows that predators did nothing, only that a before-and-after comparison cannot measure their effect. The concession must come last because it limits the doubt raised in sentence 1.

Common wrong entry 3124: Sentence 1 is wary of 'precisely this tidiness', which is the moral shape described in sentence 2; and sentence 4's 'before-and-after comparison' answers the rival causes of sentence 1, so the two cannot be separated by sentence 2.
Common wrong entry 3241: Sentence 4 concedes that the predators may have mattered; before sentence 1 there is no doubt for it to qualify.

Common misconception: Moving straight from the story in sentence 3 to the ecologists in sentence 1 because it seems natural to bring in the experts next, which leaves 'this tidiness' with nothing to refer to and strands the appeal of sentence 2 between the doubt and its qualification.

Difficulty: Order depends mainly on the logic of the argument: the story, why it appeals, why that appeal invites doubt, and a concession that limits the doubt. The tempting order brings in the sceptics too early. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-C09-345 · Para jumble · DifficultTITA
The five sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of five numbers as your answer.

1.Storytellers, though, are not copyists: they shape a tale to the audience in front of them and freely combine what they have heard from several tellers.

2.The more fruitful question may be not what the first telling was, but why certain variations took root in certain places and not in others.

3.Each retelling, on this view, was an imperfect copy of an earlier one, and the scholar's task was to strip away accumulated errors until the source emerged.

4.For a long time, scholars who collected the many versions of a familiar folk tale treated them as clues to a lost original, the form in which the story was first told.

5.Once versions mingle like this, there is no single line of descent to trace, and the family tree that the method requires gives way to something more like a web.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 43152

Sentence 4 introduces the old search for a lost original. Sentence 3 spells out the assumption behind it 'on this view': every version is a copy of an earlier one. Sentence 1 challenges that assumption with 'though': tellers are not copyists and combine versions. Sentence 5 draws the consequence of mingling 'like this': there is no single line of descent. Sentence 2 proposes a better question, which makes sense only once the search for the first telling has been shown to fail.

Common wrong entry 43125: Sentence 5 explains the consequence of tellers mixing versions 'like this', and it must follow sentence 1 directly; after sentence 2 the paragraph would propose a new question before showing why the old one fails.
Common wrong entry 41352: Sentence 3 expounds the view of sentence 4 ('on this view'); after sentence 1 has already denied that tellers are copyists, restating the copying assumption would come too late.

Common misconception: Treating sentence 2 as the natural response to sentence 1 and ending with sentence 5; but 'the more fruitful question' replaces the search for an original, and it is sentence 5 that shows that search cannot succeed.

Difficulty: Order depends mainly on the logic of the idea: a method, its assumption, the fact that defeats the assumption, the consequence for the method and the alternative question. The last two sentences tempt a swap. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-C09-346 · Para jumble · DifficultTITA
The four sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3 and 4) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of four numbers as your answer.

1.In the early years of a campaign to eradicate a disease, each round of vaccination prevents a great many cases at modest cost.

2.Measured by cases averted for each unit of money spent, the final stage can look like poor value, since the same funds might save more lives if spent on common illnesses.

3.As the disease retreats into a few remote or unsettled regions, every remaining case becomes far more expensive to prevent.

4.That comparison leaves out the one return that only eradication offers: once transmission has ended everywhere, vaccination can eventually stop and its costs end with it.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 1324

Sentence 1 describes the cheap early stage of eradication. Sentence 3 describes the expensive late stage, and the order of the two follows from the logic of a disease that 'retreats' over time. Sentence 2 judges the final stage by cost per case and finds it poor value. Sentence 4 answers 'That comparison', the cost-per-case reckoning of sentence 2, by pointing to the permanent saving it ignores. The rebuttal must close, because it overturns the verdict of sentence 2.

Common wrong entry 1342: Sentence 4 refers to 'That comparison', but the comparison of cases averted per unit of money is made only in sentence 2.
Common wrong entry 3124: Sentence 3 says the disease 'retreats', which presupposes the campaign already under way in sentence 1; and the cheap early years cannot follow a description of the costly final stage.

Common misconception: Ending with sentence 2 on the thought that 'poor value' is the paragraph's verdict; but sentence 4 begins 'That comparison', which needs the comparison of sentence 2 before it, and it reverses that verdict.

Difficulty: Order depends mainly on the logic of the idea: rising marginal cost, a cost-per-case verdict and its rebuttal. The cost-per-case verdict looks like a conclusion, tempting candidates to end on it. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-C09-347 · Para jumble · DifficultTITA
The four sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3 and 4) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of four numbers as your answer.

1.Students who fear mathematics tend to perform worse in it, and the obvious explanation is that weak skills breed anxiety.

2.A student who is busy worrying about failure has less mental room to hold numbers and steps in mind, and so makes errors that seem to confirm the fear.

3.That explanation is not wrong, but it is incomplete, for anxiety also consumes the working memory that a calculation needs.

4.The relationship thus runs in both directions, which suggests that easing the fear may improve performance even before any new skill is taught.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 1324

Sentence 1 states the link between fear and poor performance and the 'obvious explanation' that weak skills cause anxiety. Sentence 3 judges 'That explanation' incomplete and introduces the reverse mechanism, anxiety using up working memory. Sentence 2 illustrates that mechanism with a worrying student. Sentence 4 concludes that 'The relationship thus runs in both directions', which needs both causal directions to have been stated.

Common wrong entry 1234: After sentence 2 has shown worry crowding out working memory, sentence 3 would introduce that mechanism as new with 'also', and 'That explanation' would refer to the illustration rather than to the obvious explanation of sentence 1.

Common misconception: Placing the worrying student of sentence 2 directly after sentence 1 because it seems to describe the fear; but sentence 2 illustrates the claim about working memory, which sentence 3 introduces with 'also', and sentence 3 would then repeat what has already been shown.

Difficulty: Order depends mainly on the logic of the idea: one causal direction, the claim that it is incomplete, an illustration of the reverse direction, and the two-way conclusion. The illustration of the worrying student is tempting immediately after the opener. · Exams: CAT, XAT

1.None of this makes student opinion worthless; it means that the ratings answer a narrower question than the one they are often asked to settle.

2.Universities increasingly ask students to rate their teachers, on the reasonable ground that learners are the people best placed to say what a course was like.

3.That experience is an unreliable guide to how much they learned, since a demanding course may feel worse at the time than an easy one.

4.What students can report well, however, is their experience of a course: whether lectures were clear, and whether the teacher seemed prepared and approachable.

5.Ratings can therefore reward the very comfort that sometimes works against learning, especially when they are used to decide promotions.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 24351

Sentence 2 introduces student ratings and the reasonable case for them. Sentence 4 narrows what students report well, with 'however', to their experience of a course. Sentence 3 says 'That experience' is a poor guide to learning. Sentence 5 concludes with 'therefore' that ratings may reward comfort over learning, which needs the gap between experience and learning in sentence 3. Sentence 1 concedes that the ratings still have value but answer a narrower question, a qualification that must follow the criticism.

Common wrong entry 24315: Sentence 5 draws its 'therefore' from the gap between experience and learning in sentence 3; placed after the concession of sentence 1, it would make the concession the ground for the criticism.
Common wrong entry 23451: Sentence 3 begins 'That experience', which needs the experience of a course described in sentence 4.

Common misconception: Placing the concession of sentence 1 before sentence 5 because it seems to soften the critique at the right moment; but 'therefore' in sentence 5 then follows a concession instead of the reason it draws on.

Difficulty: Order depends mainly on the logic of the argument: a reasonable practice, the distinction between experience and learning, the consequence, and a closing concession. The concession tempts an earlier placement. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-C09-349 · Para jumble · DifficultTITA
The five sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3, 4 and 5) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of five numbers as your answer.

1.The two crafts share a microphone but little else, which may be why a fine radio voice does not always succeed on television.

2.For a television audience the picture already does that work, and a commentator who describes what viewers can plainly see soon becomes an irritation.

3.Each pass, each shift in position and each change in the weather has to be turned into words at the pace of play.

4.Radio commentators at a sporting event carry a burden that their television counterparts do not: the listener can see nothing except what the voice provides.

5.What the screen cannot supply is context: the history of a rivalry, the tactical reason for a substitution, the meaning of a moment within the season.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 43251

Sentence 4 sets up the contrast and states the radio commentator's burden. Sentence 3 spells out that burden: everything must be turned into words. Sentence 2 says that on television the picture 'already does that work', the work of description just described, so describing becomes tiresome. Sentence 5 names what the screen cannot do and so what the television commentator must add. Sentence 1 concludes that the crafts share little, which needs both job descriptions before it.

Common wrong entry 43521: If sentence 5 comes before sentence 2, the picture 'already does that work' would mean it supplies context, contradicting the claim that context is what the screen cannot supply.
Common wrong entry 42351: Sentence 2 says the picture 'already does that work'; the work in question is turning every pass into words, which is described only in sentence 3, and the radio burden of sentence 3 would otherwise interrupt the account of television.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 5 before sentence 2 because both concern the screen; but 'that work' in sentence 2 would then refer to supplying context, which sentence 5 says the picture cannot do.

Difficulty: Order depends mainly on the logic of a comparison: the radio task, why that task is redundant on television, what television needs instead, and the conclusion. Few connectives mark the links. · Exams: CAT, XAT

CAT-VARC-C09-350 · Para jumble · DifficultTITA
The four sentences (labelled 1, 2, 3 and 4) given below, when properly sequenced, form a coherent paragraph. Decide on the proper order of the sentences and key in the sequence of four numbers as your answer.

1.In formal prose a full stop is the most neutral of marks, telling the reader nothing except that a sentence has ended.

2.Many settle on coldness or annoyance, even when the writer was merely following habits formed in letters and essays.

3.Once leaving it out became normal, putting it in turned into a choice, and readers who notice a choice tend to look for a motive behind it.

4.A text message hardly needs that information, because each one arrives in its own bubble, and many writers have stopped bothering with the final mark.

Type your answer
Answer and explanation

Answer: 1432

Sentence 1 states the neutral function of the full stop in formal prose. Sentence 4 says a text message 'hardly needs that information', the information that a sentence has ended, so writers drop the mark. Sentence 3 follows from this: once omission is 'normal', inclusion becomes a choice that invites a search for a motive. Sentence 2 gives the motive many readers settle on, and its 'Many' refers to the readers of sentence 3.

Common wrong entry 1342: Sentence 3 says leaving the mark out 'became normal', but nothing has yet explained why it would be left out; and sentence 4's 'that information' would then refer to a motive rather than to the end of a sentence.

Common misconception: Placing sentence 3 directly after sentence 1 because it seems to continue the discussion of the mark; but omission cannot be 'normal' until sentence 4 has described why texters leave the stop out.

Difficulty: Order depends mainly on the logic of the idea: a neutral norm, a new medium that makes the mark unnecessary, the resulting shift in meaning and how readers interpret it. The links are conceptual, with few connectives. · Exams: CAT, XAT
